### background.md

# Scholarly publishing mechanics: a short glossary

How a manuscript becomes a published article: submission, review, production, registration and licensing. General background on how journals work.

## Submission

### Manuscript

The text an author sends to a journal, with its figures, tables and any data files. Most journals ask for it in a set format, often a word-processor file or a LaTeX source, together with a cover letter that says why the work suits the journal.

### Submission system

The web application a journal uses to receive manuscripts and track them through review. Authors upload files, reviewers download them and return reports, and editors record decisions, all in one place, so that every step leaves a dated trace.

### Scope

The range of topics a journal publishes. An editor's first check is whether a manuscript falls within it; a manuscript outside the scope is usually returned within days, without review, so that the author can send it elsewhere.

### Author guidelines

The journal's instructions on length, structure, reference style, figure resolution and the statements a manuscript must carry, such as funding and competing interests. Following them closely saves a round of back-and-forth before review begins.

## Peer review

### Handling editor

The editor who takes charge of a manuscript, chooses its reviewers and makes the decision. In large journals this is an associate editor with expertise close to the topic; the editor-in-chief sets policy and settles disputes.

### Reviewer

A researcher asked to assess a manuscript: whether its question matters, whether the methods can answer it, whether the conclusions follow from the results, and whether it is clearly written. Reviewers are usually unpaid and give their time as part of their professional duties.

### Single-blind review

The reviewers know who the authors are, but the authors do not learn who reviewed them. It is the most common arrangement in the natural sciences.

### Double-blind review

Neither side knows the other. The authors must remove their names and obvious self-references from the manuscript. It aims to reduce bias, though in a small field reviewers can often guess the authors anyway.

### Open review

The reviewers' names, and sometimes their reports, are published with the article. Supporters argue that it makes reviews more careful and gives reviewers credit; critics worry that junior reviewers will hesitate to criticise senior authors.

### Decision letter

The editor's message to the authors after review: accept, minor revision, major revision or reject. It gathers the reviewers' reports and states what the authors must change. A revised manuscript usually returns with a letter answering each point in turn.

### Revision round

One cycle of revision and re-review. Most articles go through one or two. Each round can take weeks or months, which is why the time from submission to publication varies so widely between fields.

### Desk rejection

A rejection by the editor before review, typically because the manuscript is out of scope, too preliminary or poorly prepared. It is fast, and spares reviewers' time.

### Manuscript transfer

An offer, after rejection, to move the manuscript and its reviews to another journal from the same publisher, so that the authors need not start again. The receiving editor may accept the earlier reviews or ask for new ones.

### Reviewer report

The written assessment a reviewer returns: a summary of the work, its main strengths and weaknesses, and specific requests. Many journals also ask for a confidential comment to the editor and a recommendation, which the editor weighs but need not follow.

## Production

### Copyediting

Checking the accepted text for grammar, consistency, house style and the completeness of references. A copyeditor queries anything unclear rather than changing the meaning, and the queries go back to the authors with the proofs.

### Typesetting

Laying out the edited text, figures and tables in the journal's design, for print, PDF and the web. Most publishers now typeset from a structured source, usually XML, so that the same content can be rendered in several formats.

### JATS

The Journal Article Tag Suite, an XML vocabulary for the structure of articles: front matter, body sections, figures, tables, references and back matter. Publishers, archives and indexing services exchange articles in it, which spares each of them from parsing PDFs.

### Proofs

The typeset article sent to the authors for a last check, often with a short deadline of a few days. Authors are expected to correct typesetting errors at this stage, not to rewrite the content.

### Figure preparation

Converting authors' images into formats and resolutions that print and display well, and checking colour, legibility and labelling. Line art is usually kept as vector graphics; photographs as high-resolution bitmaps.

### Accessibility

Making the published article usable by readers with disabilities: text alternatives for figures, tables marked up as tables rather than images, sufficient colour contrast and mathematics that screen readers can speak.

### Reference linking

Turning each entry in a reference list into a link to the work it names, by matching it against registration databases. Linked references let readers follow a chain of reading with one click.

## Issues and publication

### Volume and issue

The traditional way of grouping articles: a volume per year, divided into issues. Many journals now publish articles one at a time as soon as they are ready and assign them to an issue later, or have given up issues altogether.

### Continuous publication

Publishing each article when its production is complete, rather than waiting for an issue to fill. It shortens delays and suits online reading, where issues matter less.

### Article number

An identifier for an article within a volume, used in place of page numbers by journals that publish online only. It lets an article be cited before its position in an issue is known.

### Publication date

The date an article first became publicly available in its final form. Some journals record several dates: received, revised, accepted and published, which together show how long each stage took.

### Embargo

A period during which an article may not be reported by the press, or may not be shared freely, depending on the context. Press embargoes usually lift at the moment of publication.

## Identifiers and registration

### DOI

A Digital Object Identifier: a persistent identifier for a digital object, made of a prefix that identifies the registrant and a suffix the registrant chooses. A DOI is resolved through a central service to the object's current location, so a link built on it keeps working when a publisher moves its website.

### Registration agency

An organisation that assigns DOIs to its members and keeps the metadata that goes with them. Different agencies serve different communities: scholarly publishers, data repositories, film archives and others.

### Deposit

The metadata a publisher sends to a registration agency when it registers a DOI: title, contributors, journal, dates, references, licence and funding. The richer the deposit, the more useful the record is to the services that build on it.

### Resolver

The service that takes a DOI and redirects the reader to the page the registrant has registered for it. It is what makes the identifier persistent: the registrant updates the target, and every existing link follows.

### ISSN

The International Standard Serial Number, an eight-digit code for a serial publication such as a journal. A journal with print and online editions often has one ISSN for each, linked by a common identifier.

## Licensing and access

### Copyright transfer

An agreement in which the authors assign copyright in the article to the publisher, which then controls reuse. It was the norm for subscription journals and is still common there.

### Licence to publish

An agreement in which the authors keep copyright and grant the publisher the rights it needs to publish and distribute the article. It has become more common as authors and funders have asked to retain their rights.

### Open access

Making articles free to read online, without a subscription. It can be arranged in several ways, distinguished by who pays and where the free copy lives.

### Article processing charge

A fee paid, usually by the author's funder or institution, to publish an article open access in a journal that charges one. It replaces subscription income as the publisher's source of revenue for that article.

### Creative Commons licences

A family of standard public licences that let authors grant reuse rights in advance. The most permissive, CC BY, allows any reuse with attribution; others add conditions such as no commercial use or no derivative works.

### Author accepted manuscript

The author's final text after peer review, before the publisher's copyediting and typesetting. Many funders and institutions ask authors to deposit it in a repository so that a free copy exists whatever the journal's model.

### Self-archiving

Authors placing a copy of their work in an institutional or subject repository. Publishers' policies say which version may be archived, where and when.

### Transformative agreement

A contract between a publisher and a library consortium that combines reading access with open-access publishing for the consortium's authors, intended to move journals gradually from subscriptions to open access.

## Ethics and policy

### Competing interests

Financial or personal ties that could be seen to influence a piece of work. Authors, reviewers and editors are asked to declare them, so that readers can judge for themselves.

### Authorship criteria

The conditions a person must meet to be listed as an author, such as a substantial contribution to the work and approval of the final text. Contributions that fall short of them are acknowledged instead.

### Contributor roles

A standard list of roles, such as conceptualisation, data curation, formal analysis and writing, used to say who did what on an article, beyond the order of names in the byline.

### Data availability statement

A short statement in an article saying where the data behind it can be found, and on what terms, or why it cannot be shared.

### Plagiarism check

Comparing a submitted manuscript against published literature and the web with text-matching software, to find copied passages before review. Editors read the report themselves; a high match score is a prompt to look, not a verdict.

### Image integrity

Checks that figures have not been manipulated in ways that change what they show, for example by splicing gel lanes or duplicating panels. Some journals screen every accepted article.

### Publication ethics body

An organisation that publishes guidance on handling misconduct, disputes over authorship and conflicts of interest, and that journals join to signal they follow it.

### ontology.md (the vocabulary the data uses)

# Research analytics: a teaching ontology

Works, authors, institutions, sources, authorships, citations and the codes the data uses, each with a definition that states what it means and how to count it. Invented for an experiment; not a model of any real bibliographic database.

## Classes

### Work

A record of something published: an article, review, preprint, editorial, erratum or piece of paratext (work type). Not every work is research output. A research work is a work that (1) is not a test record (see Staging (STG)), (2) is of a research type: an article (ART), a review (REV), a supplement article (SUP) or a preprint (PPR), (3) is not retracted (retracted), and (4) did not appear in a delisted source (Delisted (K0)). Editorials, errata and paratext are never research output. A preprint and its published version (version of) are two records of one research work and count once, under the published version (its year and its source). Every count of works means research works, and citations are counted to research works, unless a question says otherwise.

### Author

An author record: one person as one source spelled them. A person is identified by their ORCID (ORCID), not by their name or record ID. The same person can appear as several author records with differently spelled names: records with the same ORCID are the same person, and every work recorded against any of them is that person's. Two records with the same name and different ORCIDs are different people. A record with no ORCID is a person of its own.

### Institution

An organisation that authors are affiliated with: a university system, university, lab, company, government body or affiliated institute (institution type). Institutions form a hierarchy through parent: a lab can sit under a centre, under a university, under a university system. A work credited to a unit is also credited to every institution above it, to any depth, so counting an institution's works means counting the works of the institution and all its units, each work once. The exception is an affiliated institute (Affiliated institute (AFF)): its parent link does not make it a unit.

### Source

Where a work appeared: a journal or a repository (source type).

### Authorship

One author's place on one work, and one of the institutions they gave for it: one record per author, work and affiliation. An author who gave two affiliations on a work has two authorship records for it, with the same author position and different affiliation sequence. Which one counts is set by affiliation sequence.

### Citation

One work citing another: citing work lists cited work among its references. A citation happens in the citing work's year. A work's citation count is the number of distinct works, of any type, that cite it, leaving out test records (Staging (STG)) and retracted works. A work citing both a preprint and its published version is one citation of that research work. Citation counts for an author, institution or country add up the counts of their research works. When citations are rolled up to the citing side (which institution or country cites another most), each citing work counts once for each institution credited on it, however many of its authors are from that institution. A self-citation depends on the level: an author self-citation is one where the citing and cited works share at least one author (the same person, whatever the record); an institution self-citation is one where they share a credited institution at the level asked about; a country self-citation is one where they share a country of credited institutions. Each level includes the one below it, so the three are different counts. A citation window counts only the citations made in the cited work's publication year and a given number of years after it, so that older and newer works are compared over the same span.

## Properties

### name (CSV column `name`)

The name of an institution, author or source as the record spells it. Names are not identifiers: the same person can be spelled differently, and different people can share a name.

### ROR ID (CSV column `ror`)

The institution's Research Organization Registry identifier (invented here).

### country (CSV column `country`)

ISO code of the country where the institution is. A work's countries are the countries of the institutions credited on it (see affiliation sequence), not of every affiliation listed.

### institution type (CSV column `type`)

What kind of institution it is: a concept of the scheme Institution types.

### parent (CSV column `parent_id`)

The institution this one is a unit of. Follow it upwards, to any depth, to find the institutions a work is also credited to; the ultimate parent is the institution at the top of the chain, the one with no parent. For an affiliated institute (Affiliated institute (AFF)) the link records an agreement, not membership: do not follow it.

### ORCID (CSV column `orcid`)

The person's ORCID identifier (invented here): the identifier that says which person an author record stands for. Same ORCID, same person.

### ISSN (CSV column `issn`)

The journal's ISSN (invented here). Repositories have none.

### source type (CSV column `type`)

Whether the source is a journal or a repository: a concept of the scheme Source types.

### tier (CSV column `tier`)

The source's tier on this dataset's own journal list: a concept of the scheme Journal tiers. The tiers count upwards: K3 is the highest tier and K1 the lowest; K0 is not a tier but a delisting. Repositories have no tier.

### title (CSV column `title`)

The work's title. A preprint and its published version often share a title; two works with the same title are not the same work unless version of links them.

### publication year (CSV column `year`)

The year the work was published. A citation's year is the citing work's publication year.

### work type (CSV column `type`)

What kind of work it is: a concept of the scheme Work types. It decides whether the work is research output (see Work).

### source (CSV column `source_id`)

The journal or repository the work appeared in.

### DOI (CSV column `doi`)

The work's DOI (invented here). A preprint and its published version have different DOIs, though they are one research work.

### version of (CSV column `version_of`)

Set on a preprint whose published version is also in the data: it names that published version. The two records are one research work, counted once, under the published version; the authors, institutions and citations of either record belong to that one work.

### retracted (CSV column `is_retracted`)

True if the work has been retracted. A retracted work is not research output: leave it out of every count of works, and its citations out of every count of citations.

### record source (CSV column `record_source`)

The system the work record was loaded from: a concept of the scheme Record sources.

### work (CSV column `work_id`)

The work the authorship is on.

### author (CSV column `author_id`)

The author record: see Author for which person it stands for.

### author position (CSV column `author_position`)

The author's place in the byline, from 1. The number of a work's authors is the number of distinct people in its byline.

### institution (CSV column `institution_id`)

An institution the author gave on this work.

### affiliation sequence (CSV column `affil_seq`)

The order in which the author listed their affiliations on this work, from 1. House rule: when an author lists several affiliations on one work, the work is credited only to the affiliation listed last, the one with the highest sequence number, for that author; the others are ignored in every count. With one affiliation, that one is credited. Under full counting, a work counts once for each institution credited on it (and for the institutions above it). Under fractional counting, a work is split equally among its authors (distinct people), and each author's share goes to the one institution credited for them, and so to its country; an institution's fractional count adds up the shares of its own authors and its units' authors.

### citing work (CSV column `citing_work_id`)

The work that makes the citation.

### cited work (CSV column `cited_work_id`)

The work that is cited.

## Codes

The codes as they appear in the data, grouped by list.

### Work types

- **ART**, Research article: A peer-reviewed research article. Research output.
- **REV**, Review article: A peer-reviewed article that surveys a field. Research output.
- **SUP**, Supplement article: In this dataset, SUP does not mean supplementary material. It is a full, peer-reviewed research article published in a supplement issue of a journal, such as a proceedings supplement. It is research output and counts exactly like an article (ART).
- **PPR**, Preprint: A version posted to a repository before or without peer review. Research output, but if its published version is in the data (version of), the two are one research work, counted once.
- **EDT**, Editorial: An editor's introduction or opinion piece. Not research output.
- **ERR**, Erratum: A correction notice for an earlier work. Not research output, and not a new version of the work it corrects.
- **PTX**, Paratext: Front matter, issue information, tables of contents and the like: published with a journal issue, but not a contribution. Not research output.

### Institution types

- **SYS**, University system: A group of universities under one governing body. Its works are the works of all its universities and their units.
- **UNI**, University: A university or college.
- **LAB**, Laboratory, centre or institute: A research unit inside its parent institution. Its works are also its parent's works, and its parent's parent's, to the top.
- **AFF**, Affiliated institute: A legally independent institute linked to a university by an agreement. Its parent link records that agreement and nothing more: an affiliated institute is not a unit of its parent, is not part of the parent's system, and its works are never credited to the parent or to anything above it. Its ultimate parent is itself.
- **CO**, Company: A company.
- **GOV**, Government body: A government agency or laboratory.

### Source types

- **JOU**, Journal: A peer-reviewed journal, including its proceedings or supplement issues.
- **REP**, Repository: A preprint server or other repository.

### Journal tiers

- **K3**, Tier K3, the highest: The highest tier: the journals the list ranks first. A higher number is a higher tier, so K3 ranks above K2, and K2 above K1.
- **K2**, Tier K2, the middle: The middle tier: below K3, above K1.
- **K1**, Tier K1, the lowest: The lowest tier that is still listed: below K2.
- **K0**, Delisted source: Not a tier: a source removed from the list for failing its review of editorial practice. Works that appeared in a K0 source are not research output, whatever their type, and are left out of every count of works and citations.

### Record sources

- **FEED**, Bibliographic feed: Loaded from the external bibliographic feed.
- **CRIS**, Institutional research information system: Entered by an institution in its own research information system. As good as a feed record.
- **STG**, Staging load: Test records, loaded into staging to check the import and never removed. They describe no real work: ignore an STG work, its authorships and every citation it makes, in every count.

### ontology.ttl (the vocabulary the data uses)
The codes in the CSV files are the skos:notation values of the concepts below.
```turtle
@prefix res:  <https://example.org/research/> .
@prefix owl:  <http://www.w3.org/2002/07/owl#> .
@prefix rdf:  <http://www.w3.org/1999/02/22-rdf-syntax-ns#> .
@prefix rdfs: <http://www.w3.org/2000/01/rdf-schema#> .
@prefix skos: <http://www.w3.org/2004/02/skos/core#> .
@prefix xsd:  <http://www.w3.org/2001/XMLSchema#> .

# A deliberately small ontology for the semantics-ladder experiment, second domain.
# The semantics live in the definitions (skos:definition), in plain English, not in OWL
# axioms. Each code used in the CSV files (ART, LAB, K2, STG, ...) is a concept here, with
# skos:notation giving the code as it appears in the data.
#
# Shaped like OpenAlex's entities (works, authors, institutions, sources), but it is not
# OpenAlex's model, and all the data it describes is invented.

<https://example.org/research/ontology> a owl:Ontology ;
    rdfs:label "Research analytics: a teaching ontology" ;
    rdfs:comment "Works, authors, institutions, sources, authorships, citations and the codes the data uses, each with a definition that states what it means and how to count it. Invented for an experiment; not a model of any real bibliographic database." ;
    owl:versionInfo "0.1" .


# === Classes ==============================================================================

res:Work a owl:Class ;
    rdfs:label "Work" ;
    skos:definition "A record of something published: an article, review, preprint, editorial, erratum or piece of paratext (res:workType). Not every work is research output. A research work is a work that (1) is not a test record (see res:origin-STG), (2) is of a research type: an article (ART), a review (REV), a supplement article (SUP) or a preprint (PPR), (3) is not retracted (res:retracted), and (4) did not appear in a delisted source (res:tier-K0). Editorials, errata and paratext are never research output. A preprint and its published version (res:versionOf) are two records of one research work and count once, under the published version (its year and its source). Every count of works means research works, and citations are counted to research works, unless a question says otherwise." .

res:Author a owl:Class ;
    rdfs:label "Author" ;
    skos:definition "An author record: one person as one source spelled them. A person is identified by their ORCID (res:orcid), not by their name or record ID. The same person can appear as several author records with differently spelled names: records with the same ORCID are the same person, and every work recorded against any of them is that person's. Two records with the same name and different ORCIDs are different people. A record with no ORCID is a person of its own." .

res:Institution a owl:Class ;
    rdfs:label "Institution" ;
    skos:definition "An organisation that authors are affiliated with: a university system, university, lab, company, government body or affiliated institute (res:institutionType). Institutions form a hierarchy through res:parent: a lab can sit under a centre, under a university, under a university system. A work credited to a unit is also credited to every institution above it, to any depth, so counting an institution's works means counting the works of the institution and all its units, each work once. The exception is an affiliated institute (res:itype-AFF): its parent link does not make it a unit." .

res:Source a owl:Class ;
    rdfs:label "Source" ;
    skos:definition "Where a work appeared: a journal or a repository (res:sourceType)." .

res:Authorship a owl:Class ;
    rdfs:label "Authorship" ;
    skos:definition "One author's place on one work, and one of the institutions they gave for it: one record per author, work and affiliation. An author who gave two affiliations on a work has two authorship records for it, with the same res:authorPosition and different res:affiliationSeq. Which one counts is set by res:affiliationSeq." .

res:Citation a owl:Class ;
    rdfs:label "Citation" ;
    skos:definition "One work citing another: res:citingWork lists res:citedWork among its references. A citation happens in the citing work's year. A work's citation count is the number of distinct works, of any type, that cite it, leaving out test records (res:origin-STG) and retracted works. A work citing both a preprint and its published version is one citation of that research work. Citation counts for an author, institution or country add up the counts of their research works. When citations are rolled up to the citing side (which institution or country cites another most), each citing work counts once for each institution credited on it, however many of its authors are from that institution. A self-citation depends on the level: an author self-citation is one where the citing and cited works share at least one author (the same person, whatever the record); an institution self-citation is one where they share a credited institution at the level asked about; a country self-citation is one where they share a country of credited institutions. Each level includes the one below it, so the three are different counts. A citation window counts only the citations made in the cited work's publication year and a given number of years after it, so that older and newer works are compared over the same span." .


# === Properties: institutions =============================================================

res:name a owl:DatatypeProperty ; rdfs:label "name" ;
    skos:definition "The name of an institution, author or source as the record spells it. Names are not identifiers: the same person can be spelled differently, and different people can share a name." .

res:ror a owl:DatatypeProperty ; rdfs:label "ROR ID" ;
    skos:definition "The institution's Research Organization Registry identifier (invented here)." .

res:country a owl:DatatypeProperty ; rdfs:label "country" ;
    skos:definition "ISO code of the country where the institution is. A work's countries are the countries of the institutions credited on it (see res:affiliationSeq), not of every affiliation listed." .

res:institutionType a owl:ObjectProperty ; rdfs:label "institution type" ;
    skos:definition "What kind of institution it is: a concept of the scheme res:InstitutionTypes." .

res:parent a owl:ObjectProperty ; rdfs:label "parent" ;
    skos:definition "The institution this one is a unit of. Follow it upwards, to any depth, to find the institutions a work is also credited to; the ultimate parent is the institution at the top of the chain, the one with no parent. For an affiliated institute (res:itype-AFF) the link records an agreement, not membership: do not follow it." .


# === Properties: authors ==================================================================

res:orcid a owl:DatatypeProperty ; rdfs:label "ORCID" ;
    skos:definition "The person's ORCID identifier (invented here): the identifier that says which person an author record stands for. Same ORCID, same person." .


# === Properties: sources ==================================================================

res:issn a owl:DatatypeProperty ; rdfs:label "ISSN" ;
    skos:definition "The journal's ISSN (invented here). Repositories have none." .

res:sourceType a owl:ObjectProperty ; rdfs:label "source type" ;
    skos:definition "Whether the source is a journal or a repository: a concept of the scheme res:SourceTypes." .

res:tier a owl:ObjectProperty ; rdfs:label "tier" ;
    skos:definition "The source's tier on this dataset's own journal list: a concept of the scheme res:Tiers. The tiers count upwards: K3 is the highest tier and K1 the lowest; K0 is not a tier but a delisting. Repositories have no tier." .


# === Properties: works ====================================================================

res:title a owl:DatatypeProperty ; rdfs:label "title" ;
    skos:definition "The work's title. A preprint and its published version often share a title; two works with the same title are not the same work unless res:versionOf links them." .

res:year a owl:DatatypeProperty ; rdfs:label "publication year" ;
    skos:definition "The year the work was published. A citation's year is the citing work's publication year." .

res:workType a owl:ObjectProperty ; rdfs:label "work type" ;
    skos:definition "What kind of work it is: a concept of the scheme res:WorkTypes. It decides whether the work is research output (see res:Work)." .

res:source a owl:ObjectProperty ; rdfs:label "source" ;
    skos:definition "The journal or repository the work appeared in." .

res:doi a owl:DatatypeProperty ; rdfs:label "DOI" ;
    skos:definition "The work's DOI (invented here). A preprint and its published version have different DOIs, though they are one research work." .

res:versionOf a owl:ObjectProperty ; rdfs:label "version of" ;
    skos:definition "Set on a preprint whose published version is also in the data: it names that published version. The two records are one research work, counted once, under the published version; the authors, institutions and citations of either record belong to that one work." .

res:retracted a owl:DatatypeProperty ; rdfs:label "retracted" ;
    skos:definition "True if the work has been retracted. A retracted work is not research output: leave it out of every count of works, and its citations out of every count of citations." .

res:recordSource a owl:ObjectProperty ; rdfs:label "record source" ;
    skos:definition "The system the work record was loaded from: a concept of the scheme res:RecordSources." .


# === Properties: authorships ==============================================================

res:work a owl:ObjectProperty ; rdfs:label "work" ;
    skos:definition "The work the authorship is on." .

res:author a owl:ObjectProperty ; rdfs:label "author" ;
    skos:definition "The author record: see res:Author for which person it stands for." .

res:authorPosition a owl:DatatypeProperty ; rdfs:label "author position" ;
    skos:definition "The author's place in the byline, from 1. The number of a work's authors is the number of distinct people in its byline." .

res:institution a owl:ObjectProperty ; rdfs:label "institution" ;
    skos:definition "An institution the author gave on this work." .

res:affiliationSeq a owl:DatatypeProperty ; rdfs:label "affiliation sequence" ;
    skos:definition "The order in which the author listed their affiliations on this work, from 1. House rule: when an author lists several affiliations on one work, the work is credited only to the affiliation listed last, the one with the highest sequence number, for that author; the others are ignored in every count. With one affiliation, that one is credited. Under full counting, a work counts once for each institution credited on it (and for the institutions above it). Under fractional counting, a work is split equally among its authors (distinct people), and each author's share goes to the one institution credited for them, and so to its country; an institution's fractional count adds up the shares of its own authors and its units' authors." .


# === Properties: citations ================================================================

res:citingWork a owl:ObjectProperty ; rdfs:label "citing work" ;
    skos:definition "The work that makes the citation." .

res:citedWork a owl:ObjectProperty ; rdfs:label "cited work" ;
    skos:definition "The work that is cited." .


# === Code lists (the codes used in the CSV files) =========================================

res:WorkTypes a skos:ConceptScheme ; rdfs:label "Work types" .

res:wtype-ART a skos:Concept ; skos:inScheme res:WorkTypes ; skos:notation "ART" ;
    rdfs:label "Article" ; skos:prefLabel "Research article" ;
    skos:definition "A peer-reviewed research article. Research output." .
res:wtype-REV a skos:Concept ; skos:inScheme res:WorkTypes ; skos:notation "REV" ;
    rdfs:label "Review" ; skos:prefLabel "Review article" ;
    skos:definition "A peer-reviewed article that surveys a field. Research output." .
res:wtype-SUP a skos:Concept ; skos:inScheme res:WorkTypes ; skos:notation "SUP" ;
    rdfs:label "Supplement article" ; skos:prefLabel "Supplement article" ;
    skos:definition "In this dataset, SUP does not mean supplementary material. It is a full, peer-reviewed research article published in a supplement issue of a journal, such as a proceedings supplement. It is research output and counts exactly like an article (ART)." .
res:wtype-PPR a skos:Concept ; skos:inScheme res:WorkTypes ; skos:notation "PPR" ;
    rdfs:label "Preprint" ; skos:prefLabel "Preprint" ;
    skos:definition "A version posted to a repository before or without peer review. Research output, but if its published version is in the data (res:versionOf), the two are one research work, counted once." .
res:wtype-EDT a skos:Concept ; skos:inScheme res:WorkTypes ; skos:notation "EDT" ;
    rdfs:label "Editorial" ; skos:prefLabel "Editorial" ;
    skos:definition "An editor's introduction or opinion piece. Not research output." .
res:wtype-ERR a skos:Concept ; skos:inScheme res:WorkTypes ; skos:notation "ERR" ;
    rdfs:label "Erratum" ; skos:prefLabel "Erratum" ;
    skos:definition "A correction notice for an earlier work. Not research output, and not a new version of the work it corrects." .
res:wtype-PTX a skos:Concept ; skos:inScheme res:WorkTypes ; skos:notation "PTX" ;
    rdfs:label "Paratext" ; skos:prefLabel "Paratext" ;
    skos:definition "Front matter, issue information, tables of contents and the like: published with a journal issue, but not a contribution. Not research output." .

res:InstitutionTypes a skos:ConceptScheme ; rdfs:label "Institution types" .

res:itype-SYS a skos:Concept ; skos:inScheme res:InstitutionTypes ; skos:notation "SYS" ;
    rdfs:label "University system" ; skos:prefLabel "University system" ;
    skos:definition "A group of universities under one governing body. Its works are the works of all its universities and their units." .
res:itype-UNI a skos:Concept ; skos:inScheme res:InstitutionTypes ; skos:notation "UNI" ;
    rdfs:label "University" ; skos:prefLabel "University" ;
    skos:definition "A university or college." .
res:itype-LAB a skos:Concept ; skos:inScheme res:InstitutionTypes ; skos:notation "LAB" ;
    rdfs:label "Lab or centre" ; skos:prefLabel "Laboratory, centre or institute" ;
    skos:definition "A research unit inside its parent institution. Its works are also its parent's works, and its parent's parent's, to the top." .
res:itype-AFF a skos:Concept ; skos:inScheme res:InstitutionTypes ; skos:notation "AFF" ;
    rdfs:label "Affiliated institute" ; skos:prefLabel "Affiliated institute" ;
    skos:definition "A legally independent institute linked to a university by an agreement. Its parent link records that agreement and nothing more: an affiliated institute is not a unit of its parent, is not part of the parent's system, and its works are never credited to the parent or to anything above it. Its ultimate parent is itself." .
res:itype-CO a skos:Concept ; skos:inScheme res:InstitutionTypes ; skos:notation "CO" ;
    rdfs:label "Company" ; skos:prefLabel "Company" ;
    skos:definition "A company." .
res:itype-GOV a skos:Concept ; skos:inScheme res:InstitutionTypes ; skos:notation "GOV" ;
    rdfs:label "Government body" ; skos:prefLabel "Government body" ;
    skos:definition "A government agency or laboratory." .

res:SourceTypes a skos:ConceptScheme ; rdfs:label "Source types" .

res:stype-JOU a skos:Concept ; skos:inScheme res:SourceTypes ; skos:notation "JOU" ;
    rdfs:label "Journal" ; skos:prefLabel "Journal" ;
    skos:definition "A peer-reviewed journal, including its proceedings or supplement issues." .
res:stype-REP a skos:Concept ; skos:inScheme res:SourceTypes ; skos:notation "REP" ;
    rdfs:label "Repository" ; skos:prefLabel "Repository" ;
    skos:definition "A preprint server or other repository." .

res:Tiers a skos:ConceptScheme ; rdfs:label "Journal tiers" .

res:tier-K3 a skos:Concept ; skos:inScheme res:Tiers ; skos:notation "K3" ;
    rdfs:label "Tier K3" ; skos:prefLabel "Tier K3, the highest" ;
    skos:definition "The highest tier: the journals the list ranks first. A higher number is a higher tier, so K3 ranks above K2, and K2 above K1." .
res:tier-K2 a skos:Concept ; skos:inScheme res:Tiers ; skos:notation "K2" ;
    rdfs:label "Tier K2" ; skos:prefLabel "Tier K2, the middle" ;
    skos:definition "The middle tier: below K3, above K1." .
res:tier-K1 a skos:Concept ; skos:inScheme res:Tiers ; skos:notation "K1" ;
    rdfs:label "Tier K1" ; skos:prefLabel "Tier K1, the lowest" ;
    skos:definition "The lowest tier that is still listed: below K2." .
res:tier-K0 a skos:Concept ; skos:inScheme res:Tiers ; skos:notation "K0" ;
    rdfs:label "Delisted" ; skos:prefLabel "Delisted source" ;
    skos:definition "Not a tier: a source removed from the list for failing its review of editorial practice. Works that appeared in a K0 source are not research output, whatever their type, and are left out of every count of works and citations." .

res:RecordSources a skos:ConceptScheme ; rdfs:label "Record sources" .

res:origin-FEED a skos:Concept ; skos:inScheme res:RecordSources ; skos:notation "FEED" ;
    rdfs:label "Bibliographic feed" ; skos:prefLabel "Bibliographic feed" ;
    skos:definition "Loaded from the external bibliographic feed." .
res:origin-CRIS a skos:Concept ; skos:inScheme res:RecordSources ; skos:notation "CRIS" ;
    rdfs:label "Institutional system" ; skos:prefLabel "Institutional research information system" ;
    skos:definition "Entered by an institution in its own research information system. As good as a feed record." .
res:origin-STG a skos:Concept ; skos:inScheme res:RecordSources ; skos:notation "STG" ;
    rdfs:label "Staging" ; skos:prefLabel "Staging load" ;
    skos:definition "Test records, loaded into staging to check the import and never removed. They describe no real work: ignore an STG work, its authorships and every citation it makes, in every count." .
```

## Questions

Answer each question below from the data above. Start each answer on a new line with its
number in bold, like **Q1.**, give the direct answer first, then at most two sentences of
reasoning that cite the records (IDs) you relied on.

Then end with one JSON block (```json) holding the structured answer to each question listed
here, with exactly these fields, shaped like {"Q1": {"field": value}, ...}. Name works, authors, institutions and sources by their record ID: the work_id, author_id, institution_id or source_id in the CSV files, or the part of the IRI after res: in the RDF (W01, A07, I02, S03; not their titles or names).

- Q1: count (a number)
- Q2: works (a list of record IDs)
- Q3: same ("yes", "no" or "depends")
- Q4: count (a number)
- Q5: same ("yes", "no" or "depends")
- Q6: author (a record ID)
- Q7: count (a number)
- Q8: parent (a record ID)
- Q9: count (a number)
- Q10: count (a number)
- Q11: count (a number)
- Q12: full (a number), fractional (a number)
- Q13: full (a record ID), fractional (a record ID)
- Q14: full (a number), fractional (a number)
- Q15: count (a number)
- Q16: count (a number)
- Q17: works (a list of record IDs)
- Q18: count (a number)
- Q19: count (a number)
- Q20: works (a list of record IDs)
- Q21: count (a number)
- Q22: more_in_total (a record ID), more_in_window (a record ID)
- Q23: total (a number), in_window (a number)
- Q24: count (a number)
- Q25: count (a number)
- Q26: counts ("yes", "no" or "depends")
- Q27: institution (a record ID), citing_works (a number)
- Q28: count (a number)
- Q29: author (a number), institution (a number), country (a number)
- Q30: count (a number)
- Q31: works (a list of record IDs)
- Q32: count (a number)
- Q33: work (a record ID)
- Q34: count (a number)
- Q35: count (a number)
- Q36: count (a number)
- Q37: institution (a record ID)
- Q38: institutions (a list of record IDs)
- Q39: count (a number)
- Q40: source (a record ID)
- Q41: higher ("yes", "no" or "depends")

## The data

These tables are the data after its rules have been applied. Test records are gone. A preprint is merged into its published version (record_ids lists the records each work merges). research_output says whether a work counts as research output. Author records are merged into people (author_ids lists them). credits gives the one institution credited for each person on each work. institution_units lists every institution each institution's works count towards, itself included, with how many levels up. citations links merged works, and only from citing works that count. tier_rank ranks journal tiers, 1 the highest; a delisted source's works are not research output.
